A Mixed Linear and Graded Logic: Proofs, Terms, and Models (with appendices)
Victoria Vollmer, Danielle Marshall, Harley Eades III, Dominic Orchard

Abstract
Graded modal logics generalise standard modal logics via families of modalities indexed by an algebraic structure whose operations mediate between the different modalities. The graded “of-course” modality ! r !_{r} captures how many times a proposition is used and has an analogous interpretation to the of-course modality from linear logic; the of-course modality from linear logic can be modelled by a linear exponential comonad and graded of-course can be modelled by a graded linear exponential comonad. Benton showed in his seminal paper on Linear/Non-Linear logic that the of-course modality can be split into two modalities connecting intuitionistic logic with linear logic, forming a symmetric monoidal adjunction. Later, Fujii et al. demonstrated that every graded comonad can be decomposed into an adjunction and a ‘strict action’. We give a similar result to Benton, leveraging Fujii et al.’s decomposition, showing that graded modalities can be split into two modalities connecting a graded logic with a graded linear logic. We propose a sequent calculus, its proof theory and categorical model, and a natural deduction system which we show is isomorphic to the sequent calculus system. Interestingly, our system can also be understood as Linear/Non-Linear logic composed with an action that adds the grading, further illuminating the shared principles between linear logic and a class of graded modal logics.

1
Introduction

Intuitionistic logic has a central role in the foundations of programming
language theory, providing a logical basis for type theories and type systems, and other
program reasoning principles. A significant amount of the
expressivity of proof systems for intuitionistic logic (both natural deduction and
sequent calculus forms) lies within the structure of the hypotheses—the

context
. Probing the foundations of this part of the logic has,
perhaps surprisingly, yielded the very fertile field of

substructural logics

[
40
]
including
influential logics such as linear logic
[
15
]
and its
variants, and the Lambek calculus
[
26
]
.

By restricting the manipulation of hypotheses in the context we typically arrive
at logics which align more closely with physical reality, where propositions are
instead ‘resources’ that cannot necessarily be copied, discarded, or reordered.
Such restricted logics have been used to construct type systems for
safely manipulating values that should be treated in a resourceful way, such as file handlers, pointers
to mutable memory, or channels
[
43
,
42
]
.

However, such pervasive restrictions often hamper expressivity
and thus some substructural logics then seek to carefully control the reintroduction of structural rules.
For example, linear logic provides the
!
modality (‘of course’) for
reintroducing weakening and contraction of propositions, which linear logic
otherwise prohibits. However, this modality is coarse-grained: for those
propositions under the modality, it re-enables all the structural rules that
have been removed in linear logic.
Subexponentials
instead aim to be
more fine-grained, offering families of modalities capturing specific structural
rules
[
8
,
23
]
. The related notion of

grading

[
13
,
32
]
gives an alternate view,
providing an indexed family of modalities whose indices are subject to an
algebra which accounts for any structural rules applied: structural rules are
‘counted’ by the algebra (whose operations mirror the shape of structural
rules). Bounded Linear Logic
[
16
]
is a special case where
the family of modalities
!_{n}A
uses indices
n
which are natural numbers
(or polynomial terms over naturals) counting the upper bound on usage of the
proposition
A
. Various systems generalise this approach to arbitrary semirings
to capture data-flow
properties
[
1
,
2
,
5
,
13
,
14
,
25
,
29
,
32
,
34
,
35
,
37
]
.
Such graded systems annotate hypotheses/variables in the context with elements
of the semiring (‘grades’) denoting their usage, e.g.,
x:_{0}A\vdash t:B
types a
term
t
which does not use
x
and
y:_{1+1}A\vdash t^{\prime}:B
types a term
t^{\prime}

in which
y
is used in two different subterms once each, accounted for by the semiring
addition. A graded modality
internalises
the semiring grade, causing a multiplication
to the grades of any captured dependencies when the graded modality is
introduced, e.g.,
y:_{0*(1+1)}A\vdash\Box t^{\prime}:\Box_{0}B
.

We seek here to further
understand the underlying structure of graded modal logics by following
an ‘adjoint resolution’ approach à la Benton’s seminal
“A Mixed Linear and Non-Linear Logic: Proofs, Terms and Models” at CSL 1994
[
3
]
.
Benton showed that the exponential modality of linear logic (modelled by a
comonad) can be decomposed into an adjunction, defining a pair of ‘adjoint’ logics (a linear logic and a
non-linear intuitionistic, or “Cartesian”, logic) which embed into each other
[
3
]
. This provides a beautiful reduction of
the core features of linear logic and its non-linearity modality.

Adjoint logic
applies the same idea but to
subexponentials
[
38
,
39
]
.
We follow the same scheme, via the adjoint decomposition of graded
modalities which generalise linear logic’s
!
and which are traditionally modelled by graded exponential comonads
[
5
,
6
,
13
,
14
,
25
,
35
]
.
Whilst Benton’s work has a pair of adjoint modalities mediating between the two sublogics, we have a
pair of a modality
\mathsf{Lin}
and a
graded modality

\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}
. We give a categorical model, showing that these are captured by an LNL-like adjunction paired with a ‘strict action’ for incorporating the grading, following the Fujii-Katsumata-Melliès adjoint decomposition of graded (co)monads
[
10
,
25
]
.
The result is a pair of logics which serve to explain and clarify
the relationship between linearity and grading. We call our system
Mixed Graded/Linear (
\mathsf{mGL}{}
) Logic.

This pair of logics also shines light on a relationship between two
styles of graded system in the literature: those which take
linear types as their basis augmented with a graded modality
[
6
,
13
,
32
]
versus those with no base
notion of linearity where grading is pervasive, tracking all substructurality
[
1
,
2
,
4
,
7
,
29
,
31
,
35
]
.
Our linear fragment is analogous to the former whilst our graded fragment is analogous
to the latter. The mutual embedding shows that these two styles of
graded logics have a similar relationship to the adjoint relationship of intuitionistic
logic and linear logic.

Aside from the internal motivation of better understanding the
relationship between grading and linearity, an external motivation for
this work is that it can provide a basis for flexible, safe programming with
resources. By separating out the linear fragment from
an intuitionistic graded fragment, one could avoid the strictures of
linearity for working with standard data types which need
not be linear, working only in the linear fragment for handling
resources like file handles. The mutual embedding would allow the programmer to
move smoothly between these two subcalculi, as seen
also in other adjunction-based calculi, e.g., for concurrent programming
[
36
]
.
The focus here however is on the core
theory rather than developing these applications yet.

Since our focus is on the relationship between grading and linearity,
we consider the semiring-graded modalities that generalise linear logic’s
!
.
Other flavours of graded modality (e.g., graded
monads for capturing side effecting behaviour
[
24
,
33
]
) are not
considered here.

Roadmap

Section
2
defines a pair of sequent calculi,
the mixed fragment
\mathsf{MS}{}
, which has both linear and graded assumptions, and the graded
fragment
\mathsf{GS}{}
, which has only graded assumptions and no function arrow. As described
above, these calculi have a mutual embedding via modalities between the two.
Section
3
considers the categorical model
of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
leveraging recent work on the adjoint resolution of graded
comonads
[
10
,
25
]
. Section
4

provides the natural deduction formulation of the calculus, which is proved
equivalent to the sequent calculus version. Section
5

discusses how this work gives a view on the landscape of graded systems in the literature and considers other related work and future applications.

2
Mixed Graded/Linear Logic: Proofs and Terms

\displaystyle\hskip-18.99995pt\begin{array}[]{rl}(\mathsf{GS}{}/\mathsf{GT}{})\quad t::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathsf{j}\mid\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{j}\,=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\textit{graded}\qquad\quad\;\mid&(\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\mid\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\mid&\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{l}\\[40.00006pt]
\end{array}\;\begin{array}[]{rl}(\mathsf{MS}{}/\mathsf{MT}{})\quad l::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathsf{i}\mid\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{i}\,=\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\;\textit{linear}\qquad\quad\;\;\mid&(\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\mid\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\mid&\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{l}\mid\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\mid&\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{t}\mid\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\mid&\mathsf{Unlin}\,\mathit{z}\\
\mid&\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{j}\,=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}\mid\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}\end{array}

Variables are ranged over by
x
,
y
,
z
in both fragments.

Terms are mostly grouped above with introduction forms followed by elimination forms,
though note that in the last two lines of syntax for
\mathit{l}
there are additional eliminators: for the linear modality (
\mathsf{Unlin}
), for units
\mathsf{j}
, and for tensors coming from the graded context.

Figure 1:
Collected term syntax

We present first a sequent calculus for Mixed Graded/Linear logic, which comes in the form of a
term assignment. Figure
1
collects the term syntax for reference; it will also be used in Section
4
for the natural deduction formulation.
The syntax is explained with reference to its associated proof rules in the
next section.

Benton’s approach has two proof systems
[
3
]
: one system
of linear propositions (the L of LNL) with two contexts for linear and
non-linear propositions respectively, and one system of non-linear
propositions (the NL in LNL). We generalise this approach to the
graded setting by replacing the non-linear parts with
graded
notions.
Thus, our system (
\mathsf{mGL}{}
) has two analogous proof systems: one of
linear propositions with two contexts for linear and graded propositions,
with judgments subscripted as
\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}
(for ‘Mixed (linear/graded) Sequent’),
and one system of graded propositions, with judgments
subscripted as
\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}
(‘Graded Sequent’).

The syntax of Benton’s propositions is split into two, ‘
conventional
’ (i.e.,
Cartesian / non-linear) and
linear

[
3
]
.
The syntax of our propositions is analogously split into two,
graded
and
linear
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{(Graded)}&\mathit{X},\mathit{Y},\mathit{Z}::=\mathsf{J}\mid\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y}\mid\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A}\\
\text{(Linear)}&\mathit{A},\mathit{B},\mathit{C}::=\mathsf{I}\mid\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}\mid\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}\mid\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\\
\end{array}

where
\mathsf{J}
and
\mathsf{I}
are unit types, and
\boxtimes
and

\otimes
are tensor (product) operators in their respective
domains. In the case of the linear domain, the product is the
standard multiplicative conjunction. The
\mathsf{Lin}
modality
encapsulates a linear proposition as a graded proposition, and the

\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}
modality encapsulates a graded proposition (at
grade
r
, whose structure is defined below) as a linear proposition. Thus, the two logics
are interconnected by
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}

and
\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A}
. Using these two modalities we will later define graded modalities
\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}
as
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,(\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A})
, similarly to how the of-course modality
\oc\mathit{A}
can be
defined in LNL logic as the composition of two adjoint modalities.

Definition 1
.

Grades (ranged over by
r,s
) are drawn from a semiring parameterizing the system

(\mathcal{R},1,*,\mathsf{0},+,\leq)
with preorder
(\mathcal{R},\leq)
such that both
*
and
+
are monotonic wrt
\leq
.

The semiring governs the structural rules: the additive part of the semiring is
involved in weakening and contraction, and the multiplicative part in usage and
composition. Various concrete examples of interesting semirings are given at the
end of Subsection
2.1
.

Section
4
develops an equivalent
natural deduction formulation of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
. We then show that the natural
deduction and the sequent calculus are interderivable without modifying the term
witnessing a derivation. Thus, any semantic model of one is a model of the
other. We opt to focus on the sequent calculus form for now without loss
of generality.

2.1
Sequent Calculus

We first define contexts used in the judgments:

Definition 2

(Graded contexts)
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{R},1,*,\mathsf{0},+,\leq)
is a preordered semiring (Def.
1
). Then
grade
vectors

\delta
are sequences of
\mathcal{R}
, contexts
\Delta
are sequences of
graded formulas
\mathit{X}
, and contexts
\Gamma
are sequences of linear
formulas:

\displaystyle\delta:=\emptyset\mid\delta,\mathit{r}\qquad\;\;\Delta:=\emptyset\mid\Delta,\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\qquad\;\;\Gamma:=\emptyset\mid\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}

The comma operator is overloaded for sequence concatenation, i.e., we can write

\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, which further requires that

\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are disjoint contexts.

A
graded context

\delta\odot\Delta
is a pairing of a grade vector
and a context defined as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cll}\emptyset\odot\emptyset=\emptyset\qquad\;\;(\delta,\mathit{r})\odot(\Delta,\mathit{x}:\mathit{X})=(\delta\odot\Delta),x:(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X})\end{array}

where
\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}
pairs a formula with a grade
r
capturing (by the rules
of the system) how the formula
X
(named
x
) is used to form a judgment.

We lift the operations of semirings to grade vectors,
forming a semimodule, with the
pointwise addition and scalar multiplication defined in a standard way:

\displaystyle\emptyset+\emptyset

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathit{r}*\emptyset

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}})+(\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}),(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}})

\displaystyle\mathit{r}*(\delta,\mathit{s})

\displaystyle=(\mathit{r}*\delta),(\mathit{r}*\mathit{s})

Addition of grade vectors requires the vectors to be of the same length.

The judgment form for our fully graded logic
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}

captures a concluding proposition
X
under the graded context
of assumptions
\delta\odot\Delta
. Mixed graded/linear logic judgments

\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}
are similar but also have a context
\Gamma
of
linear assumptions which, being linear, do not have a corresponding grade vector.

The two judgments
\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}
and
\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}
(also called
sub-logics
or
fragments
) are
defined by mutual induction. We present conceptually related rules
from both systems side-by-side where possible, or one-after-the-other, in the
order
\mathsf{GS}
then
\mathsf{MS}
.

The identity (axiom) rules are:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{}{1\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{id${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}&&\quad\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset;\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{id${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\end{array}

The multiplicative identity
1
is the ‘default’ grade
for formulas in the graded logic
\mathsf{GS}{}
(left), in the sense that we
can think of the right-hand side of the judgment as also
implicitly having grade
1
. The graded identity rule says that
a graded formula that is used must have the default grade. For
example, in the natural number semiring
(\mathbb{N},1,*,0,+,=)
the
multiplicative identity
1\in\mathbb{N}
captures linear usage.
The mixed identity rule types linear assumption use, requiring just a singleton
linear context (forcing a lack of weakening). It also
requires that there are no graded
formulas in context—the graded context is empty
\emptyset
.

The ‘cut’ rules are:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r}*\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cut${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}&\\[7.5pt]
\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cut${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}&\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r}*\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{gcut${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\end{array}

The
cut
GS
rule provides a cut through a graded proposition
X

of grade
r
in the receiving context (second premise). Thus, the resulting
term uses semiring multiplication (lifted to contexts, Def.
2
) to capture sequential usage, scaling the grade vector

\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
of the cut term
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
by
\mathit{r}
.
The
cut
MS
rules provides a cut through a linear proposition
A

and has no effect on the graded contexts. However,
\mathsf{MS}
has
a further cut rule
gcut
MS
for graded propositions
in its graded context, incurring a scaling similarly to
cut
GS
.
This pattern occurs throughout: operations applied to the graded context
in
\mathsf{GS}
have a sister rule in
\mathsf{MS}
applying the same operation
in the
\mathsf{MS}
graded context.

Both sub-logics have free use of exchange:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{ex${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{ex${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\quad\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{Y},\mathit{y}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{gex${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\end{array}

Exchanging graded propositions simultaneously exchanges their
grades in the grade vector.

We can use weakening and contraction in the graded system and the mixed system
within the graded contexts, with the semiring’s
0
representing weakened
hypotheses and the grades of contracted hypotheses combined
via semiring addition
+
:

\displaystyle\hskip-5.0pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathsf{0},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{weak${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}&\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}[\mathit{x}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cont${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathsf{0},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{weak${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}&\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{x}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cont${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\end{array}

The left and right rules for units for the graded
and mixed logics are akin to linear logic:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathsf{J},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{j}\,=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{J}}_{L}$}{},}&\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{j}:\mathsf{J}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{J}}_{R}$}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathsf{I},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{i}\,=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{I}}_{L}$}{},}&\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{i}:\mathsf{I}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{I}}_{R}$}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathsf{J},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{j}\,=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{J}-\mathsf{MS}}_{L}$}{},}\end{array}

Thus, in
\mathsf{GS}
, we can eliminate a graded unit
\mathsf{j}

at an arbitrary grade
r
, whereas the linear unit
\mathsf{i}
in

\mathsf{MS}
gets eliminated from the linear context. The
additional left rule (
\text{unit}^{\mathsf{J}-\mathsf{MS}}_{L}
) for

\mathsf{MS}
again similarly eliminates graded units
\mathsf{J}
in the graded context.

Tensor products are then eliminated in each fragment as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\boxtimes_{L}$}{},}\;\,\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}(\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\boxtimes_{R}$}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\otimes_{L}$}{},}\;\,\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}(\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\otimes_{R}$}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\boxtimes_{L-\mathsf{MS}}$}{},}\end{array}

The left rule for
\boxtimes
eliminates from the graded context
at any grade
r
, where the components of the tensor product both inherit
this grade in the premise. Reading instead top-down,
the graded tensor product requires that both components are graded with
the same grade; this is similar to linear products, where both components are
linear.

Note that Benton has two left rules for (non-linear) tensor products, in the ‘projection’ style. We instead must use the pattern matching style for the
soundness of grading so that each component is bound to a variable with the same grade.

Only the mixed linear-graded system has implication, and only on linear
propositions, thus we have
\multimap
in
\mathsf{MS}
with left and right rules:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{C}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{z}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{C}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\multimap_{L}$}{},}\qquad\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\multimap_{R}$}{},}\end{array}

In Lemma
4
, we recover a graded
implication through the modal operators of the system in the same way
that Melliès did for (ungraded) LNL logic
[
30
]
.

We now consider the modal operators
\mathsf{Lin}
and
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}

which connect the two sub-logics.

The right rule for the
\mathsf{Lin}
modality transports a linear formula from the
linear system
\mathsf{MS}
into the graded system
\mathsf{GS}
where it can be
reasoned with non-linearly as accounted for by grading. The
corresponding left rule is akin to

dereliction
from linear logic, enabling a linear assumption
x:A
to
treated as a (renamed) graded assumption
z:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A}
at grade
1
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta,1)\odot(\Delta,\mathit{z}:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathsf{Unlin}\,\mathit{z}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Lin}_{L}$}{},}\qquad\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{l}:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Lin}_{R}$}{},}\end{array}

The other modal operator
\mathsf{Grd}
, or rather the family
of modal operators
\mathsf{Grd}_{r}
, transports a graded
formula with its grade into the linear system where it can be reasoned
with linearly:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{(\delta,\mathit{r})\odot(\Delta,\mathit{x}:\mathit{X});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}{\delta\odot\Delta;(\mathit{z}:\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X},\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Grd}_{L}$}{},}\qquad\frac{\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}{\mathit{r}*\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{t}:\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Grd}_{R}$}{},}\end{array}

The right rule is akin to
promotion
for
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}
where
we subsequently scale the graded context by the grade
r
. The left
rule ‘unboxes’ a graded modality
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}
providing access to the

\mathit{X}
formula ‘inside’, graded at
\mathit{r}
.

Perhaps the most remarkable property of these modal operators is that
they decompose semiring-graded necessity modalities into
\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}=\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,(\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A})

[
25
]
within the mixed system. In fact, their
introduction and elimination rules are derivable:

Lemma 3

(
\mathsf{mGL}{}
Graded Necessity Modality)
.

The following are derivable:

\displaystyle\hskip-17.50002pt\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\Box_{E}$}{},}\,\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}{(\mathit{r}*\delta)\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,(\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{l}):\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\Box_{i}$}{},}\end{array}

Proof.

The elimination rule follows by applying
\mathsf{Grd}_{L}

to the second premise and then applying cut with the first
premise. The introduction rule follows by

\mathsf{Lin}_{R}
then
\mathsf{Grd}_{R}
.
∎

The previous lemma reveals a lot about the structure of existing graded type
systems. First, graded hypotheses, usually denoted with their grade
\mathit{x}:_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}
in
the literature (e.g.
[
1
,
32
]
), are
graded hypotheses
\mathit{r}\odot\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A}
where the linear formula has been transported
into the graded system. Second, the restriction to only graded variables in the
promotion rule is very explicit in the introduction rule

\Box_{i}
above. Third, the elimination rule (here

\Box_{E}
) is really the left rule for
\mathsf{Grd}
followed by a cut.
Thus, graded type systems, whilst typically of a natural deduction form,
incorporate a little of the flavour of sequent calculi in the rules for graded
modalities because of the integrated cut.

Using the modal operators we can derive a graded implication of the form
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\multimap\mathit{A}
:

Lemma 4

(
\mathsf{mGL}{}
Graded Implication)
.

The following rules are derivable:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r}*\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\multimap\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{z}\,(\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{t})/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\multimap_{GL}$}{},}\\[5.0pt]
\frac{(\delta,\mathit{r})\odot(\Delta,\mathit{x}:\mathit{X});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}{\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\lambda\mathit{y}.(\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{y}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}):\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\multimap\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\multimap_{GR}$}{},}\end{array}

Proof.

The left rule follows by applying
\mathsf{Grd}_{R}
to
the first premise, and then
\multimap_{L}
using
the second premise. The right rule follows by applying

\mathsf{Grd}_{L}
to the premise and then

\multimap_{R}
.
∎

The final rules are the approximation rules
for grades:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{sub${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\quad\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{sub${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\end{array}

Approximation allows for the abstraction of grades along an ordering. For
example, in the semiring
(\mathbb{N},1,*,0,+,\leq)
where the
order is the usual ordering on natural numbers, then a grade
\mathit{r}
stands for “at-most
\mathit{r}
”,
generalising the notion of ‘affine’ usage tracking. Disabling the ordering
by forcing it to be true only in the case of reflexive pairs (i.e., an equality
relation) results in exact usage tracking. This demonstrates that having a notion of
approximation results in a more general usage tracking framework.

Example 5

(Derivation)
.

As an example derivation in the natural numbers semiring
(\mathbb{N},1,*,0,+,\leq)
,
for ‘affine’ usage
tracking, the following copies an assumption to make a pair in the graded fragment,
then uses an approximation, and then transports the pair into the linear fragment,
scaling its grades further:

\displaystyle\vskip-9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\,}{1\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{flushleft,right=$\textsc{id${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\;}{1\odot\mathit{y}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{y}:\mathit{X}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$\textsc{id${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}\hskip-46.2497pt}{\frac{1,1\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}):\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{X}}{\frac{2\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}[\mathit{x}/\mathit{y}](\mathit{x},\mathit{y}):\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{X}\hskip 4.5pt2\leq 3}{\frac{3\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{x}):\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{X}}{2*3\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X};\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{Grd}\,2\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{x}):\mathsf{Grd}_{2}\,(\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{X})}\hskip 4.5pt\textsc{right=$$\mathsf{Grd}_R${}$}}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{right=$\textsc{sub${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$\textsc{cont${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=$$\boxtimes_R${}$}

Example 6

(None-One-Tons
[
29
]
)
.

The
semiring over
\{0,1,\omega\}
with

0\leq\omega
and
1\leq\omega
, where
+
and
*
are saturating at
\omega
,
can be used to distinguish between
linear and various non-linear uses: assigning
\mathit{r}=1
to linear usage,

\mathit{r}=0
to non-usage (when a resource is discarded), and
\mathit{r}=\omega

to arbitrary usage.

Note, however, that even with the above semiring we are unable to exactly
represent the exponential modality
\oc
from linear logic via some particular grade
r

within the graded logic. This is because no matter which grade we
choose, we are able to “push” the grade into the tensor product using this
graded tensor elimination (
\boxtimes_{L}
), allowing
derivation of
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,(\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y})\multimap(\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\otimes\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{Y})
, and yet in linear logic it
is not possible to derive
\oc(A\otimes B)\multimap\oc A\otimes\oc B
.
Therefore, our logic cannot reduce to Benton’s LNL logic simply by taking the
Cartesian (trivial) semiring, as one might expect at first glance. This quality
is typical of
graded base
systems, so reconciling these with linear logic
requires some additional structure on the semiring
[
19
]

(though this is not the focus here).

On the other hand, notice that we have another way to represent graded products: as
linear products wrapped in the derived graded modality, or
\Box_{\mathit{r}}(\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B})
.
Importantly, here it is not possible to ‘push’ the grade ‘through’ the tensor as
we can for the graded product; we cannot derive
(\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A})\otimes(\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{B})
. This representation of graded products thus has behaviour more typical of a

linear base
graded type system, with our combined logic again giving us a
clearer understanding of the relationship between these contrasting styles.

Example 7

(Security levels)
.

Information-Flow Control properties can be tracked by instantiating
the semiring with a lattice of security levels
[
13
]
, e.g.,
with
(\{\mathsf{Lo}\leq\mathsf{Hi}\},\mathsf{Lo},\wedge,\mathsf{Hi},\vee)
where
\mathsf{Hi}
-graded inputs are treated as
irrelevant: we cannot depend on any high-security inputs
when building a low-security graded output
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathsf{Lo}}A
.

Example 8

(Sensitivity)
.

The real number semiring
(\mathbb{R},1,*,0,+,\leq)
can be
leveraged to capture a notion of numerical sensitivity in
programs/logic
[
11
,
9
]
, where
a program is
k
-sensitive (for
k\in\mathbb{R}
) in a variable if a change

\epsilon
in its inputs to
x
produces at most a change of
k\epsilon

in the output of the program. This instantiation of the system tracks
sensitivities as grades where additional dependent-type-based
mechanisms are needed to lift program values into the types, e.g.,
\mathsf{scale}:(k:\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathsf{Grd}_{k}\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

2.2
Metatheory

\mathsf{mGL}{}
enjoys a rich metatheory. First, it satisfies cut elimination,
for which we give the full proof. The proof of cut reduction requires
a generalization of the graded cut rules to graded
multicut
rules in
order to accommodate the structural rule of graded contraction.
1
1

1

Whilst
cut reduction can be proved for intuitionistic sequent
calculus without multicut
[
41
]
, we use the standard
multicut approach as it relates well to the categorical models developed later.

Thus, throughout the cut elimination proof we use the following graded
multicut rules:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta,\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}^{\mathit{n}}:\mathit{X}^{\mathit{n}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},(\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}]),\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\ldots,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{x_{\mathit{n}}}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{mcut}}{},}\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta,\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X}^{\mathit{n}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},(\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}]),\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{t},\ldots,\mathit{t}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{x_{\mathit{n}}}]\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{gmcut}}{},}\end{array}

Both rules compute the contraction of the
n
hypotheses
involved in the multicut on the cut-formula
\mathit{X}
. To do this we use
row-vector
matrix multiplication
. We denote the matrix consisting of

\mathit{n}
-copies of the row vector
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
by
[\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}^{\mathit{n}}]
. Then row-vector multiplication is:

\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}]=\bigoplus^{\mathit{n}}_{\mathit{k}=1}(\delta(\mathit{k})*\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})

where the
*
on the right is the scalar multiplication derived
from the semiring,
\bigoplus
is the pointwise addition of
vectors, and where
\delta(\mathit{k})
is the
k
-th element of the vector

\delta
. This computes the usages of the hypotheses in

\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
as the multiplication of a matrix of size

1\times n
with a
matrix of size
n\times|\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}|
to yield a matrix of size
1\times|\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}|
.

We now proceed with the proof of cut elimination. The
rank

\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{X})
and
\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{A})
of a formula is the height of the input
formula’s syntax tree where constants are of rank
0
. The

cut rank

\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)
of a derivation
\Pi
of some
judgment is defined to be one more than the maximum rank of the cut
formula’s in
\Pi
, and
0
if
\Pi
is cut free. The

depth

\mathsf{Depth}(\Pi)
of a derivation
\Pi
is the length
of the longest path in the proof tree of
\Pi
, and hence, the
depth of an axiom is
0
. We prove cut-elimination without term
annotations on the rules, in keeping with traditional proofs.

Lemma 9

(Cut Reduction for
\mathsf{mGL}{}
)
.

1.

(Graded) If

\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is a proof of
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
and

\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is a proof of
(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta,\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X}^{\mathit{n}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{Y}
with

\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}),\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}})\,\leq\,\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{X})
, then
there exists a proof
\Pi
of

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}],\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{Y}
with
\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)\,\leq\,\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{X})
.

2.

(Graded/Mixed) If

\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is a proof of
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
and

\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is a proof of
(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta,\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X}^{\mathit{n}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{B}
with

\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}),\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}})\,\leq\,\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{X})
, then
there exists a proof
\Pi
of

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}],\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{B}
with
\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)\,\leq\,\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{X})
.

3.

(Mixed) If

\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is a proof of
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}
and

\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is a proof of
\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{B}
with

\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}),\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}})\,\leq\,\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{A})
, then
there exists a proof
\Pi
of

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{B}
with
\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)\,\leq\,\mathsf{Rank}(\mathit{A})
.

Proof.

By mutual induction on
\mathsf{Depth}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}})+\mathsf{Depth}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}})

(see Appendix
C.1
for proof).
∎

Lemma 10

(Decreasing Order of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
)
.

If
\Pi
is a proof of
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
or
\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}

with
\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)\,>\,0
, then there is a proof
\Pi^{\prime}
of

\delta^{\prime}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
or
\delta^{\prime}\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}
with

\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi^{\prime})\,<\,\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)
.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathsf{Depth}(\Pi)
(see
Appendix
C.2
for proof).
∎

Theorem 11

(Cut Elimination of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
)
.

If
\Pi
is a proof of
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
or
\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}

with
\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)\,>\,0
, then there is an algorithm which yields
a cut-free proof
\Pi^{\prime}
of
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
or

\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}
respectively.

Proof.

Follows immediately by induction on

\mathsf{CutRank}\,(\Pi)
and the previous lemma.
∎

Lemma 12

(Subformula property)
.

1.

(Graded) Every formula occurring in a cut-free proof
\Pi
of a judgment,
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
,
consists of subformulas of the formulas occurring in
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{X}
.

2.

(Mixed) Every formula occurring in a cut-free proof
\Pi
of a judgment,
\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}
,
consists of subformulas of the formulas occurring in
\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{A}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Pi
(See
Appendix
C.3

for proof).
∎

Lastly, we define an equational theory for
\mathsf{mGL}{}
:

Definition 13

(Equational theory
\equiv
, subset)
.

An equational theory on derivations accounts for
equalities between proofs of the same sequent
arising from the graded structure (where the terms
are the same but the structure of the proof tree
differs), as well as cut elimination,
i.e., in
\mathsf{GS}
, if cut elimination on
derivation
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
of
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}

yields the cut-free derivation of
\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
for
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}:\mathit{X}

then the equational theory has
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and similarly for
\mathsf{MS}{}
.

As a
sample of two equations from the
\mathsf{GS}
fragment, the following shows an
equation leveraging the commutativity of contraction, and another on
the interaction between weakening and contraction leveraging the left-unit
of semiring addition:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}\multirow{3}{*}{$ $\frac{\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},0,\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=$\textsc{weak${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},0+\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}$\hskip 10.22217pt{right=$\textsc{cont${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}$}&\\
&\equiv&\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}\\
&&\hskip 130.0002pt\textnormal{\large{({contr-unitL})}}\\
\\
\multirow{3}{*}{$
$\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\mathit{s},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{s},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{s}+\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=$\textsc{cont${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}}$\hskip 10.22217pt{right=$\textsc{ex${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}$}&&\multirow{3}{*}{$
$\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\mathit{s},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r}+\mathit{s},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}$\hskip 10.22217pt{right=$\textsc{cont${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}$}\\
&\equiv&\\
&&\end{array}

(
contr-sym
)

Appendix
A.1
gives the full definition of the equational theory.

Remark 14

(“
\beta\eta
-equalities” and “Triangle identities” via cut reduction)
.

One might wonder where
\beta
-equalities are in the above
equational theory, e.g., that
(\lambda x.l)l^{\prime}
in

\mathsf{MS}
is equal to the cut
[l^{\prime}/x]l
. Such
\beta
-equalities are provided by the cut elimination procedure, which
reduces away interacting pairs of right and left formulas (the
principal vs. principal cases).

Similarly,
\eta
-equalities are equivalent to the identity
expansion part of cut elimination procedure (where the cut of
an identity axiom is transformed into an interacting
left and right pair, with identity axioms expanded towards the leaves).

The internal derivations for the graded equivalent of the ‘triangle
identities’ (that one usually has associated with an adjunction)
are also handled in the cut elimination procedure. The main feature
of the derivations for both identities is that after one step the left and right rules for the
modal operators match up. This leads to consecutive principal vs. principal
cases where rules for the interacting left and right pairs in the two subproofs are
removed by the reduction step.

3
Model

We detail a denotational model for
\mathsf{mGL}{}
which is based on an
adjoint decomposition of graded comonads. We introduce key definitions
as needed.

A
graded comonad
can be
summarised as a colax monoidal functor

\Box:\mathcal{I}{}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\ [\mathcal{C}{},\mathcal{C}{}]

where
\mathcal{I}{}
is a preordered monoid

(\mathcal{I},1,*,\leq)

treated as a monoidal category
and
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
is the category of endofunctors on
\mathcal{C}

[
34
,
35
]
.
Colax monoidality of
\Box
means that the laws of a monoidal functor become 2-cells, providing the graded comonad operations:

which are thus natural transformations

\varepsilon_{A}:\Box_{1}\mathit{A}\rightarrow\mathit{A}
.
and

\delta_{r,s,A}:\Box_{(\mathit{r}*\mathit{s})}\mathit{A}\rightarrow\Box_{\mathit{r}}(\Box_{\mathit{s}}\mathit{A})
.

Fujii et al.
[
10
]
gave a formal theory for graded monads,
which can be easily dualised to graded comonads,
showing that in an analogous way to an ordinary comonad, every graded
comonad can be decomposed into an adjunction

\mathsf{Mny}\dashv\mathsf{Lin}:\mathcal{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}

and (key to
graded
comonads) a monoidal action

\odot:\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
, and thus vice
versa:

Lemma 15
.

(Resolution of a graded comonad
[
25
,
10
]
)

An adjunction
\mathsf{L}\dashv\mathsf{R}:\mathcal{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
and a strict monoidal action
\odot:\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
together induce a graded comonad over the family of endofunctors defined by

\Box_{r}=\mathsf{L}(r\odot(\mathsf{R}-)):\mathcal{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{M}
.

Along with some additional structure relating to substructurality
(see below), this result provides a model of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
with
\mathcal{C}
providing
a model for
\mathsf{GS}
derivations,
\mathcal{M}
providing a model
for
\mathsf{MS}
derivations, the type constructor
\mathsf{Grd}_{r}
transporting
from
\mathsf{GS}
to
\mathsf{MS}
modelled
by
\mathsf{L}(r\odot-):\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
,
and type constructor
\mathsf{Lin}
transporting
\mathsf{MS}

to
\mathsf{GS}
modelled by
\mathsf{R}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

However we need additional structure for the (sub)structural
behaviour of our logic. In the literature on graded modal type theories,
graded comonads are extended to
graded exponential comonads
(sometimes called

graded linear exponential comonads

[
25
]
)
defined as a colax monoidal functor
\Box:\mathcal{R}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\ [\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}]

where
\mathcal{R}
is a preordered semiring
(\mathcal{R},1,*,\mathsf{0},+,\leq)

(viewed as a category),
[\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}]
is the
category of symmetric lax monoidal endofunctors on a symmetric
monoidal category
\mathcal{M}
, and
\Box
has additional
symmetric lax monoidal structure for the additional
monoidality of
\mathcal{R}
and
[\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}]

[
13
]
.
This additional structure provides natural transformations
w_{A}:\Box_{\mathsf{0}}\mathit{A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>1
and
c_{r,s,A}:\Box_{(\mathit{r}+\mathit{s})}\mathit{A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(\Box_{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A})\otimes(\Box_{\mathit{s}}\mathit{A})
capturing (graded) weakening and contraction, subject
to comonoidal coherence conditions.
This additional structure can be induced by the adjoint decomposition
given an
exponential action
:

Definition 16

(Exponential action)
.

Given a preordered semiring
(\mathcal{R},1,*,\mathsf{0},+,\leq)
and a
symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{J},\boxtimes)
, we
say that a bifunctor

\odot:\mathcal{R}\times\mathit{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
is

1.

a
strict action
(a
strict graded comonad
), if it
satisfies the following equalities:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rrll}\varepsilon_{X}:&1\odot\mathit{X}&=&\mathit{X}\\
\delta_{X,r,s}:&(\mathit{r}*\mathit{s})\odot\mathit{X}&=&\mathit{r}\odot(\mathit{s}\odot\mathit{X})\end{array}

Note that we treat these equalities as strict natural transformations
named
\varepsilon
and
\delta
;

2.

symmetric lax monoidal
in the second argument if it has:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rrll}\qquad m_{\mathsf{J},r}:&\mathsf{J}&\rightarrow&\mathit{r}\odot\mathsf{J}\\
m_{\boxtimes,r,X,Y}:&(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X})\boxtimes(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{Y})&\rightarrow&\mathit{r}\odot(\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y})\end{array}

where
m_{\mathsf{J}}
is the unit of
m_{\boxtimes}
and
m_{\boxtimes}
is associative
and commutative up to isomorphism;

3.

symmetric colax monoidal
between
(\mathcal{R},0,+,\leq)
and

(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{J},\boxtimes)
in the first argument if it has natural transformations:

\begin{array}[]{rrll}\mathsf{weak}_{X}:&0\odot\mathit{X}&\rightarrow&\mathsf{J}\\
\mathsf{contr}_{r,s,X}:&(\mathit{r}+\mathit{s})\odot\mathit{X}&\rightarrow&(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X})\boxtimes(\mathit{s}\odot\mathit{X})\end{array}

where
\mathsf{weak}
is the unit of
\mathsf{contr}
, e.g.

\rho_{r\odot X}\circ(id\boxtimes\mathsf{weak}_{X})\circ\mathsf{contr}_{r,0,X}=id

with right unitor
\rho
, and

\mathsf{contr}
is associative and commutative, i.e.,
that
\mathsf{contr}_{r,s,X}=c\circ\mathsf{contr}_{s,r,X}
.

Furthermore,
these natural transformations must be preserved by the strict
action and monoidal structure as described by the standard additional equations
in Figure
2
.

\\
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.74725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{contr}_{\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}},{\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-55.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 98.33603pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
