149.33871pt\raise-47.91669pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.35141pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}},r,X}\ \boxtimes\ \delta_{\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}},r,X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 149.33871pt\raise-55.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-62.66936pt\raise-63.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\odot(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}))\boxtimes(\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 86.66936pt\raise-63.88892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\odot(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}))\boxtimes(\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\odot(\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Figure 2
:
Further
equations of a strict exponential action, interacting the colax symmetric
monoidal structure, strict action, and (strict) monoidality.

If we have all of the above properties then we refer to
\odot
as an
exponential action
. This terminology recalls the

exponential action
of Brunel et al.
[
6
]
which is
the same as the above but where strictness is instead
laxness in their definition. Our definition is also similar to linear exponential graded comonads (see e.g.,
[
25
,
13
]
), but here the graded comonad is uncurried (in the form of an action) and has equalities for its natural transformations (strictness).

We define a
strict exponential action
to be an exponential action as above but where the monoidal structure

m_{\mathsf{J}}
and
m_{\boxtimes}
is also strict, where for clarity (in the appendix) we sometimes orient the equality as a morphism, where in the opposite direction we denote these morphisms by
n_{\mathsf{J},r}
and
n_{\boxtimes,r,X,Y}
respectively. Strictness of the monoidal structure
is needed for soundness of our model.

We now give the definition of the model of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
, where we now
use the opposite category
{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{op}}
to capture the correct
polarity of the approximation rules.

Definition 17

(Mixed Graded/Linear model)
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{J},\boxtimes)
and
(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{I},\otimes)

are symmetric monoidal
categories, where
\mathcal{M}
is symmetric monoidal closed (with exponents
\multimap
),
and
(\mathcal{R},1,*,\mathsf{0},+,\leq)
is a preordered semiring.
Then a
Mixed Graded/Linear model
is a symmetric monoidal adjunction

\mathsf{Mny}\dashv\mathsf{Lin}:\mathcal{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
along with an exponential action

\odot:{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
.

Thus an
\mathsf{mGL}{}
model is essentially an LNL model with a strict action.
However, whilst Benton’s LNL models are initially stated to require
that
\mathcal{M}
is Cartesian closed, he goes on to show that
Cartesian properties are induced for the Eilenberg-Moore
category of
!
-coalgebras for a symmetric monoidal category
[
3
]
.
In our setting, the Cartesian structure is not needed since the

\mathsf{MS}
logic is a mix of graded and linear logic,
rather than Cartesian and linear logic. That is, graded propositions
do not have arbitrary weakening and contraction, but instead these
structural rules are controlled by grades (and corresponding
underlying categorical structure
[
13
,
25
]
). Therefore,
a symmetric monoidal closed
\mathcal{M}
suffices.

From Definition
17
, we define our denotational
model of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
:

Definition 18

(Interpretation of Mixed Graded/Linear Logic.)
.

Given a Mixed Graded/Linear model (Def.
17
) (with
\mathsf{Mny}\dashv\mathsf{Lin}:\mathcal{M}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
and
\odot:{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
), we interpret by two mutually
defined interpretations
\llbracket-\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}
and
\llbracket-\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}{}}

on types and proofs (derivations):

•

For every
\mathsf{GS}{}
type
X
there is an object
\llbracket\mathit{X}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}\in\mathcal{C}
and
for every
\mathsf{MS}{}
type
A
there is an object
\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\in\mathcal{M}
, mutually defined inductively as:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rl}\llbracket\mathsf{J}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}&=\mathsf{J}\\
\llbracket\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}&=\llbracket\mathit{X}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}\boxtimes\llbracket\mathit{Y}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}\\
\llbracket\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}&=\mathsf{Lin}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\\
&\end{array}\quad

\displaystyle\quad\begin{array}[]{rl}\llbracket\mathsf{I}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}&=\mathsf{I}\\
\llbracket\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}&=\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\otimes\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\\
\llbracket\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}&=\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\multimap\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\\
\llbracket\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}&=\mathsf{Mny}(r\odot\llbracket\mathit{X}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}})\end{array}

•

For every proof
\Pi
of a
\mathsf{GS}{}
sequent

(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{r_{\mathit{n}}})\odot(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{x_{\mathit{n}}}:\mathit{X_{\mathit{n}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}

there is a morphism in the category
\mathcal{C}
:

{\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}}}:(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\odot\llbracket\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}})\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes(\mathit{r_{\mathit{n}}}\odot\llbracket\mathit{X_{\mathit{n}}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\llbracket\mathit{X}\rrbracket

(where an empty context is interpreted as
\emptyset^{\mathsf{GS}}=\mathsf{J}
).

•

For every proof
\Pi
of an
\mathsf{MS}{}
sequent

(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{r_{\mathit{n}}})\odot(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{x_{\mathit{n}}}:\mathit{X_{\mathit{n}}});\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{y_{\mathit{m}}}:\mathit{A_{\mathit{m}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}

there is a morphism in the category
\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle{\llbracket\Pi\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}}:\mathsf{Mny}(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\odot\llbracket\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathsf{Mny}(\mathit{r_{\mathit{n}}}\odot\llbracket\mathit{X_{\mathit{n}}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{GS}})\otimes\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\llbracket\mathit{A_{\mathit{m}}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{MS}}

(where an empty
\mathsf{MS}{}
context is interpreted as
\emptyset^{\mathsf{MS}}=\mathsf{I}
).

Appendix
C.4
gives the full definition of the
interpretation, including intermediate derivations from the

\mathsf{mGL}{}
model.

Finally, we have our soundness and completeness theorems:

Theorem 19

(Soundness of Mixed Graded/Linear Logic models)
.

Suppose a mixed graded/linear model as above.
Then for derivation

\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
of

\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}

and derivation

\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
of

\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{X}

then if
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}

then
\llbracket\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rrbracket
.

Similarly for
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
of

\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}

and derivation

\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
of

\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}

then if
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}

then
\llbracket\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rrbracket
.

Proof.

This proof holds by mutual induction. For the details see Appendix
C.5
.
∎

Theorem 20

(Completeness of Mixed Graded/Linear Logic models)
.

For derivations

\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
(of either
\mathsf{GS}
or
\mathsf{MS}
)
if
\llbracket\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rrbracket
in all mixed graded/linear models, then

\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Proof.

This is a standard proof,
where we build a generic model based on the syntax and the equational theory.
For the details see Appendix
C.6
.
∎

4
Natural Deduction

We now develop a natural deduction formulation of
\mathsf{mGL}{}
. Whilst sequent
calculus judgments were denoted
\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}
and
\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}
, natural
deduction judgments are correspondingly
\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}
and
\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}
.

The syntax for terms is identical to the sequent calculus, collected in Figure
1
.
Appendix
B
gives the introduction
and elimination rules and structural rules for
\mathsf{mGL}{}
’s natural deduction
formulation. The unit constructors are
\mathsf{j}
and
\mathsf{i}
. Tensor
products in both systems are denoted by pairs of terms with corresponding
let-expressions for eliminators. The graded modal introduction form
\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{l}

operates on mixed terms, dual to
\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{t}
which operates on graded
terms. The mixed syntax includes abstraction
\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{l}
and
function application
\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

The most interesting aspect is the rules for the modal
operators:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{l}:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Lin}_{I}$}{},}&\;\frac{\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{A}}{\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathsf{Unlin}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Lin}_{E}$}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}{\mathit{r}*\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{t}:\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Grd}_{I}$}{},}&\;\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X}\hskip 8.19447pt(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{Grd}\,\mathit{r}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Grd}_{E}$}{},}\end{array}\end{aligned}

In the sequent calculus presented in
Section
2
, the right rule for
\mathsf{Lin}
is in
the graded subsystem, but the left rule is in the mixed subsystem. A similar
idea arises here, the introduction rule for
\mathsf{Lin}
(rule

\mathsf{Lin}_{I}
) is in the graded subsystem and the elimination rule
(rule
\mathsf{Lin}_{E}
) is in the mixed subsystem. Introducing
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}
formulas (rule
\mathsf{Grd}_{I}
) has the effect of scaling the input
grades by
\mathit{r}
. The elimination rule for
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}
(rule

\mathsf{Grd}_{E}
) is a pattern match on the form of
\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
. Since

\mathsf{Lin}
and
\mathsf{Grd}
are the decomposition of graded modalities
(Section
3
), the form of the elimination rule
for
\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}
is defined in a way which resembles that of elimination rules
for graded modalities in other natural deduction-based type
systems
[
32
]
.

This formulation also has explicit graded structural rules:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{l}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathsf{0},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{weak}}{},}\quad\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}[\mathit{x}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cont}}{},}\\[7.5pt]
\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{ex}}{},}\end{array}\end{aligned}

In the transition from sequent calculus to natural deduction, left rules
transform into elimination rules, and as a result the additional graded
left rules in the mixed sequent calculus are no longer explicitly part of the
system, but can be derived. We go on to prove that the sequent calculus
of Section
2.1
is equivalent to the natural
deduction system.

We give two main results related to the natural deduction system; the first is
substitution for typing.
Note that this reuses the row-vector multiplication operation of
Section
2.2
.

Lemma 21

(Substitution for
\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}
and
\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}
)
.

The following hold by mutual induction:

1.

(Graded) If
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}
and

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta,\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}^{\mathit{n}}:\mathit{X}^{\mathit{n}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}
, then

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}],\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\ldots,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{x_{\mathit{n}}}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}
.

2.

(Graded/Mixed) If
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}
and

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta,\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}^{\mathit{n}}:\mathit{X}^{\mathit{n}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}
, then

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta\boxast[{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{n}}],\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}});\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}[\mathit{t},\ldots,\mathit{t}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{x_{\mathit{n}}}]\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}
.

3.

(Mixed) If
\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and

\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}
, then

(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}[\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}
.

Proof.

By mutual induction on the second assumed derivation (see
Appendix
C.7
).
∎

Since we have an explicit structural rule for contraction (above and
listed in Appendix
B
), the
substitution lemma on the graded fragment is formalized as multi-substitution.
Otherwise, its proof is a fairly standard substitution proof for graded
systems (e.g., as in
[
32
]
). Lastly, the natural deduction system
is interderivable with the sequent calculus, which we establish such that the
term witnessing the derivations does not change between systems:

Theorem 22

(Sequent calculus and natural deduction
interderivability)
.

\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}\Leftrightarrow\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GT}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}

and
\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}\Leftrightarrow\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MT}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}
.

Proof.

By mutual induction on the assumed derivations (Appendix
C.8
and
C.9
).
The sequent calculus to natural
deduction direction requires the substitution lemma above.
∎

The implication of the previous result is that we only need a semantic model of
one of the two systems, and the other can be modelled using the same
interpretation of terms. We chose to model the sequent calculus form directly.

5
Discussion

5.1
Relating linear base vs. graded base calculi

A major thread of graded type systems in the literature starts with a
linear logic base and then generalises the
!
modality to a
semiring-graded modality atop a linear logic, e.g., the systems of Brunel et
al.
[
6
]
, Gaboardi et al.
[
13
]
, Orchard
et al.
[
32
]
, and others
[
12
,
19
]
. Often these systems are
presented with a single context containing
both linear and graded propositions
[
13
,
32
]
. Overall,
these approaches have a common core which is isomorphic to the natural deduction
\mathsf{MT}
fragment shown here
with the (natural deduction analogue of the) derived
\Box_{r}
graded modality of Lemma
3
as part of their
definition (i.e., not derived).
We refer to this style of graded type system as the
linear base
style.

A contrasting approach has no base notion of linearity, but instead has pervasive
grading tracking substructurality, i.e., no linear assumptions, every assumption has a grade, and function arrows
come equipped with a grade describing the usage of their input in the function (e.g., written
A\xrightarrow{r}B
). Such
systems include the coeffect calculi of Petricek et
al.
[
34
,
35
]
, the general
graded modal system of Bernardy et al.
[
1
]
, and several
others
[
2
,
4
,
7
,
29
,
31
]
. The
\mathsf{GT}{}
fragment
of our system here corresponds to a common subset of these approaches: a subset
without function arrows and without a graded modality,
since there is no graded modality that lives in the
\mathsf{GT}{}
side (
\Box_{i}
is derived into
\mathsf{MT}{}
). Hughes et al. also develop
a program synthesis technique for graded base systems, where grades are used
to prune the search space
[
20
]
; its synthesis calculus formulation
resembles closely
\mathsf{GS}
.

Our work thus shows the relationship between the linear base and graded base style, namely that there is a mutual
embedding between these two approaches which generates the graded modality
in the linear base (Lemma
3
). Exploring this in more depth is further work. For instance, it is unclear
what is needed to realise a graded comonadic modality in
\mathsf{GT}{}
that arises from the embedding (or a different embedding), and how this could
interact with a graded function arrow in
\mathsf{GS}
or
\mathsf{GT}
. Pursuing this line of work would help to
explain the relationship between the two dominant styles of graded system in the literature, which seem strongly related, and their relative expressive power. Nonetheless,
by following Benton’s programme and giving it a graded rendering here, we can already see here the close connection between these two
styles of graded system.

5.2
Related work on adjoint logics

Pruiksma et al. formalized a general way to add and remove structural
rules from a logic through adjunctions
[
38
]
. Their work is
similar to ours as it relates logics through adjoint decompositions based on modal
operators to control structural rules. Their formulation with “modes of truth”
resembles our work with grades; however, modes of truth lack the algebraic properties
graded formulations depend on and instead have a very relational flavor.
Building on this work of Pruiksma et al., Jang et al. develop
a natural deduction formulation of adjoint logic
[
22
]
.
They leverage this to give a functional language able to reason about
resource properties like strictness and erasure. Similar reasoning
can be developed ontop of our natural deduction formulation here, though
this is left as further work.

A question is whether grading can be unified with
the adjoint logic approach. Eades and Orchard sketched a unification
based on generalising semiring operations to
relations rather than functions, with predicates classifying unit
values
[
21
]
.
Hanukaev et al. develop this idea further,
introducing a dependent type system based on a similar
structure as the logics here but using a generalised notion of
grading that combines the modes of adjoint
logic
[
17
]
. They prove that their system is
well-formed syntactically, but do not introduce any semantic
model. Our logic
\mathsf{mGL}{}
can be seen as an instantiation of their
system, but the categorical model given here could potentially be
generalised into a model of their system.

5.3
Further work

Practical implementation to leverage linear/grading separation

The separation of the mixed system (
\mathsf{MS}
/
\mathsf{MT}
) from
the purely graded fragment (
\mathsf{GS}
/
\mathsf{GT}
) (which acts more as a standard
intuitionistic system) can provide a basis for a programming
language design. In such a language, the restrictions of linearity could be
used only for handling data that needs to be linear, such as file
handles or channels. However, for data types which need not be linear,
e.g., primitive types like integers, characters, or structures
over them, the graded fragment could be used without having to confront
linearity constraints. The mutual embedding would allow the programmer to
move smoothly between these two subcalculi. Similar ideas
are discussed for the polarized extension of SILL for concurrent programming
[
36
]
. The implementation could borrow ideas from
the Granule programming language, which already provides a mature
and feature rich implementation of a linear-base style graded type system
[
32
]
. Instead, an
\mathsf{mGL}{}
-inspired implementation could be
based on the natural deduction term calculus with the modalities mediating
between the two judgments. Exploring this application, perhaps as an extension to Granule,
is future work.

Other generalisations

In LNL, the adjunction can be followed in the opposite
direction to derive a monad
?A=\mathsf{Lin}(\mathsf{Mny}A)
. However,
in
\mathsf{mGL}{}
we do not get a graded monad by composing
\mathsf{Lin}\,(\mathsf{Grd}_{\mathit{r}}\,\mathit{X})

since the adjoint resolution of a graded monad has a strict action
on the other side (on
\cal{M}
in the model). Exploring a calculus
with a pair of actions to allow both graded monads and graded comonads is further work.

Uniqueness typing

Recent work has demonstrated that

uniqueness
is a closely related but distinct concept to linearity
[
28
]
; uniqueness logic
[
18
]
is substructural in much
the same way as linear logic, but provides a monadic modality for enabling the
structural rules in contrast to linear logic’s comonadic
\oc
modality.
Building an adjoint model for uniqueness or a calculus which
unifies uniqueness and linearity
[
28
]
would be
interesting further work, and this could potentially be extended to more recent
systems which develop graded notions of uniqueness
[
27
]
.

We are also grateful for discussions with Peter Hanukaev and helpful
comments from Paulo Torrens on a draft of this manuscript.

grant EP/T013516/1 (
Verifying Resource-like Data Use in Programs via
Types
). The second author received support through Schmidt Sciences, LLC.

Appendix A
Collected rules for the
\mathsf{mGL}{}
sequent calculus

\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{r}\odot\mathit{X}

(Graded System)

\frac{}{1\odot\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{id${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{j}:\mathsf{J}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{J}}_{R}$}{},}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathsf{J},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{j}\,=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{J}}_{L}$}{},}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}\quad\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}(\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\boxtimes_{R}$}{},}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{X}\boxtimes\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\boxtimes_{L}$}{},}\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{l}:\mathsf{Lin}\,\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\mathsf{Lin}_{R}$}{},}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{X}\quad(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r}*\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cut${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathsf{0},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{weak${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}[\mathit{x}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{cont${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{y}:\mathit{Y},\mathit{x}:\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Z}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{ex${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}\quad\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{sub${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}}{},}

\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}

(Mixed System)

\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset;\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{id${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}\quad\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={{sub${}_{\mathsf{MS}}$}}{},}\frac{}{\emptyset\odot\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{i}:\mathsf{I}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{I}}_{R}$}{},}\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathsf{I},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,\mathsf{i}\,=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\text{unit}^{\mathsf{I}}_{L}$}{},}

\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}

(Mixed System (continued))

\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{B}}{\delta\odot\Delta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{l}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\multimap_{R}$}{},}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\quad\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{C}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}[\mathit{z}\,\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{C}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\multimap_{L}$}{},}\frac{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}{\delta\odot\Delta;(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathsf{let}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{l}:\mathit{C}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\otimes_{L}$}{},}\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\quad\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}};\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash_{\mathsf{MS}}(\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{l_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={$\otimes_{R}$}{},}\frac{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\odot(\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}});\Gamma\vd A.1
Equational theory

If cut elimination on
derivation
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
of
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}

yields the cut-free derivation of
\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
for
\delta\odot\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}:\mathit{X}

then
\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and similarly for
\mathsf{MS}{}
.
Below gives the remaining equalities.

Given an inequality
\mathit{r}\leq\mathit{s}
then we write:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{s},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}\quad\mathit{r}\leq\mathit{s}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{right=$\textsc{sub${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}

as syntactic sugar for:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{s},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}\quad\dfrac{\mathit{r}\leq\mathit{s}}{(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})\leq(\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{s},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}})}\textsc{cong}}{\delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{r},\delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\odot\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{X},\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{GS}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{Y}}\quad\textsc{right=$\textsc{sub${}_{\mathsf{GS}}$}{}$}
